What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been having a hard time staying on what I’m doing. For the past year or so, even small things—like my phone buzzing, someone walking past my office, or just some random thought popping into my head—can pull me off task before I even realize it. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. When I’m supposed to sit still—like at a family dinner or a church volunteer meeting—I feel like there’s a motor running inside me. I’ll start moving around or messing with something in my hands because just sitting there feels hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll jump in before I really think about it—like cutting into my wife’s conversations with her parents, or finishing an employee’s thought before they’re even done talking. I don’t mean to be rude, but I can tell it rubs people the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty completing tasks or assignments at work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, at work mostly. I’ll start a planning document or sit down with one of my managers, and I’m with it for the first few minutes. But then I’ll realize I’ve missed a good chunk of what they said, and I have to ask them to go back over it. It makes finishing things take longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble controlling your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Looking back, even as a kid, teachers were always moving my seat because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking. I remember blurting out answers before they even finished the question.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity your whole life, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there most of my life. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but it does cause problems. At home, I can tell it’s starting to affect trust when family plans don’t go the way they should. And at work, people seem to have less patience with me when meetings or mentoring conversations get off track. So… yes, it’s causing some strain.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes. Like when I’m supposed to just sit still—family dinners, or church volunteer meetings—it feels like there’s a motor running in me. I’ll jump in before I really think about it—like cutting into my wife’s conversations with her parents, or finishing an employee’s thought before they’re even done talking. It makes finishing things take longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble controlling your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get frustrated like anyone else, but I haven’t really noticed a big problem with losing control of my emotions.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I’m still keeping up overall. It’s more that at home, when family plans don’t go the way they should, it costs me trust. So it’s not completely stopping me, but it is definitely affecting things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. And at work, people can lose patience with me in meetings or mentoring conversations when I’m too restless or jump in too quickly. So it’s not ruining everything, but it is affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant problems at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, I’m still keeping up overall, but it has caused some problems. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but it is affecting both areas.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have significantly impaired your ability to function at work or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s starting to, but not to the point where I can’t function. I’m still keeping up overall. It’s more that at home, when family plans don’t go the way they should, it costs me trust. And at work, in meetings or mentoring conversations, people can lose patience with me. It’s hard to just sit calmly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been having a hard time staying on what I’m doing. For the past year or so, even small things—like my phone buzzing, someone walking past my office, or just some random thought popping into my head—can pull me off task before I even realize it. When I’m supposed to sit still—like at a family dinner or a church volunteer meeting—I feel like there’s a motor running inside me. I’ll start moving around or messing with something in my hands because just sitting there feels hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll jump in before I really think about it—like cutting into my wife’s conversations with her parents, or finishing an employee’s thought before they’re even done talking. I don’t mean to be rude, but I can tell it rubs people the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty completing tasks or assignments at work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, at work mostly. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been having a hard time staying on what I’m doing. For the past year or so, even small things—like my phone buzzing, someone walking past my office, or just some random thought popping into my head—can pull me off task before I even realize it. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. When I’m supposed to sit still—like at a family dinner or a church volunteer meeting—I feel like there’s a motor running inside me. I’ll start moving around or messing with something in my hands because just sitting there feels hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll jump in before I really think about it—like cutting into my wife’s conversations with her parents, or finishing an employee’s thought before they’re even done talking. I don’t mean to be rude, but I can tell it rubs people the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty completing tasks or assignments at work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, at work mostly. I’ll start a planning document or sit down with one of my managers, and I’m with it for the first few minutes. But then I’ll realize I’ve missed a good chunk of what they said, and I have to ask them to go back over it. It makes finishing things take longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble controlling your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get frustrated like anyone else, but I haven’t really noticed a big problem with losing control of my emotions.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Looking back, even as a kid, teachers were always moving my seat because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking. I remember blurting out answers before they even finished the question.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity your whole life, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there most of my life. Looking back, it was happening when I was a kid too—teachers would move my seat because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking. I’d also blurt things out before they finished the question.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused you significant problems or distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it has, though I’m still functioning overall. And at work, people seem to have less patience with me when meetings or mentoring conversations get off track. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been having a hard time staying on what I’m doing. For the past year or so, even small things—like my phone buzzing, someone walking past my office, or just some random thought popping into my head—can pull me off task before I even realize it. When I’m supposed to sit still—like at a family dinner or a church volunteer meeting—I feel like there’s a motor running inside me. I’ll start moving around or messing with something in my hands because just sitting there feels hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll jump in before I really think about it—like cutting into my wife’s conversations with her parents, or finishing an employee’s thought before they’re even done talking. I don’t mean to be rude, but I can tell it rubs people the wrong way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty completing tasks or assignments at work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, at work mostly. I’ll start a planning document or sit down with one of my managers, and I’m with it for the first few minutes. But then I’ll realize I’ve missed a good chunk of what they said, and I have to ask them to go back over it. It makes finishing things take longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble controlling your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get frustrated like anyone else, but I haven’t really noticed a big problem with losing control of my emotions.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Looking back, even as a kid, teachers were always moving my seat because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking. I remember blurting out answers before they even finished the question.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity your whole life, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there most of my life. Looking back, it was happening when I was a kid too—teachers would move my seat because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking. I’d also blurt things out before they finished the question.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with attention and impulsivity caused you significant problems or distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it has, though I’m still functioning overall. And at work, people seem to have less patience with me when meetings or mentoring conversations get off track. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but it is affecting both areas.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have significantly impaired your ability to function at work or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s starting to, but not to the point where I can’t function.
